# Changelog

All notable changes to GB Energy Monitor are recorded here, newest first.

Dates are shown as `yyyy-mm-dd` (UTC). Build tags use the format `YYMMDD.N`
(UTC date + increment). The server
(`grid_server.py`) and dashboard (`grid_dashboard.html`) are versioned
independently; where a change touches only one, the file and its build number are
noted on the entry.

Entries from 2026-08-17 onward carry precise build numbers. Older entries were
reconstructed from development history and are dated by build range rather than
exact tag.

**Per-file history.** Each file is versioned independently and has its own change history. The per-file build histories, formerly kept in the file headers, are in [Per-file build history](#per-file-build-history) at the end of this file. The dated entries below summarise changes across files.

---

## 2026-11-25

- **The grid-event strip under the power-cuts plots is gone.** It drew raised-to-cleared spans from the dashboard's alert journal in a lane under *Customers off / active incidents* and *Rate*, shaded the plot where the pointer crossed one, and added its titles to the hover. In practice the marks almost never lined up with anything happening in the cuts — a frequency excursion or a margin notice and a storm taking out feeders are largely unrelated events that happened to share an axis, and a coincidence of timing invited a causal reading that wasn't there. Removed with it: `loadEvents()` and its five-minute `/api/alert-history` poll, `eventsIn` / `drawEventStrip` / `eventsAt`, the key above the first plot, and the sentence about the strip in the by-operator pop-up. (`powercuts_page.html` 261125.1)

- **Yesterday's grid replaces the “today” windows, and the heat scale stops lying about severity.** The *today* view listed the windows each group is forecast to spend over its limit; the day grid shows the same thing with every half-hour visible rather than summarised, so the panel was carrying two presentations of one fact. *Today* is now the grid, and the freed tab holds **yesterday's** — the more useful of the two, because it is a completed day rather than a forecast for one still running. Order is oldest first: **yesterday · today · now**, with *yesterday* disabled until the server has the day before.

  **The colours were backwards past the limit.** The old ramp went pink at 100 % and then *darkened* through crimson to near-black over 250 %, so the worst half-hours of the day looked like the quietest ones — “pink to dark red doesn't work too well for less to more”, and it didn't. The new ramp gets brighter the whole way up, measured: luminance 21 → 38 → 65 → 91 under the limit, 99 → 135 → 187 over it. Under the limit stays cool (dim slate to a clear teal); crossing the limit flips the hue to warm, with every warm step brighter than the brightest cool one, so the crossing and the severity both read without consulting the key.

  `wall.py` now fetches from **yesterday** 00:00 rather than today's, and `_grid_for()` builds a grid for each day; about 1,600 rows and roughly 1.8 kB of payload. (`grid_wall.html` 261125.2, `wall.py` 261125.1)

- **The model was right, and the published limit was not the operative one.** NESO's metered Scotland → England transfer for 24 November scores our modelled figure at **+75 MW mean error, 177 MW mean absolute, ratio 1.018** over 43 half-hours — and through the afternoon, when the panel was reading 141–176 %, we were *under*-reading by 300–450 MW. So the transfer really did sit at 5.0–5.2 GW for hours against a published day-ahead SCOTEX limit of 3,450 MW. The percentage was telling the truth about the figures and a falsehood about the grid: it is a statement about NESO's day-ahead publication, not about how hard the boundary is being pushed.

  So the **one figure with an earned error bar now carries it**: the Scotland → England caption reads “±177 MW vs metered (43 hh)”, and no other boundary gets one, because no other boundary's flow is published and inventing error bars would undo the point. And the fold under a NESO percentage now says plainly that the day-ahead limit is a **forecast of the limit**, that the operative one is not published and can be well above it, and gives 24 November as the worked example.

  Two cautions recorded in the code with it: the error is on **half-hour means** — on 24 November the per-minute figure's median half-hour spread was 209 MW and one half-hour swung 1.8 GW, so the instantaneous number on screen is noisier than its error bar — and the score always **lags a day**, because NESO publishes the metered split up to a day late. (`grid_wall.html` 261125.1)

## 2026-11-24

- **The weakest number stops leading.** “We're effectively fudging the constraint figures” — half right, and the half that was right mattered. The turn-down is measured (notified levels less NESO-instructed levels, SO-flagged) and the transfer is conservation-determined from measured injections; the **limit** is the weak link, and it was the number being shouted loudest. On four of six boundaries it is the **ETYS 2025 capability**: a planning figure for an assumed network, which an outage can move by a gigawatt.

  So the order is reversed. Each boundary now leads with **modelled transfer** and **turn-down**, and the limit comparison comes last. Where it is NESO's own day-ahead limit the percentage is still the figure and keeps its colour; where it is the ETYS capability the **capability itself** is the figure, plainly stated and uncoloured, with the percentage demoted to its caption as “planning figure, not today's limit · modelled flow is 122 % of it”. An amber badge driven by a 2025 study is a claim we cannot support.

  **The map label changes with it.** The box near each end of a constrained boundary carried the percentage; it now carries the **measured turn-down** — ↓ 3.2 GW — with the percentage beside it only on B6 and B4. And `BND_HOT_ETYS` is gone: the doubled thickness and faster flash are reserved for NESO-limit boundaries at 100 %. That threshold was invented from no evidence yesterday and raising it to 1.15 did not make it sounder.

  Also, since the “all” list makes it easy to read down the column: the turn-down figures are **nested, not additive** — these boundaries enclose one another, so largely the same Scottish plant is counted behind each. The note says so. (`grid_wall.html` 261124.12)

- **The Western Link share does not do what I said it did.** Asked whether B6 at 141 % was a concern, I traced the percentage to NESO's SCOTEX limit, which is 3,450 MW flat today against a 4,450 MW median last week — a gigawatt lower, so the same transfer reads far higher. I then claimed the low limit also inflates our own numerator, because `wlShare()` sets the Western Link's flow from that same limit, and offered to fit the share against the metered history.

  **Working through the arithmetic before building it showed the claim was wrong.** In a DC load flow the total crossing the border IS the Scottish net injection: the share decides how that total is divided between the link and the AC circuits, not how big it is. So the share cannot move the boundary's percentage, and fitting it against the modelled-versus-metered check would have been fitting the wrong parameter. A bias in the transfer comes from the Scottish balance — the fitted demand share, the embedded estimate, the unit levels — all of which are already scored. The reasoning is now a comment at `wlShare()` and in `_b6_check()`, since it is an easy mistake to make twice.

  **What was worth doing was done.** `B6_LOG_DAYS` 4 → 30, so there is something to fit on rather than yesterday's score; and each run now logs the AC and the estimated Western Link parts beside the total (`[total, ac, dc]`), because the total alone cannot say whether an error sits in the AC flows or in the one term that is a guess. `_b6_check()` reads both the old bare numbers and the new triples and reports the mean Western Link share. (`grid_wall.html` 261124.11, `wall.py` 261124.2)

- **Constraint activity, made visible two ways.** Clicking boundary after boundary on the transfer panel to see what was happening was the wrong shape for data that changes every minute.

  **All of them at once.** The transfer panel's buttons gain **all**: every constrained boundary, ordered **north to south** by its own mean latitude, each shown exactly as the single-boundary view shows it — the same three figures, the same captions, and the same counting, tinted numbers. It was first built as a row of static bars per boundary, which told you less than the figures being clicked through one at a time, which was the point of the exercise. The list is rebuilt from `BNDON` on every refresh, so it grows and shrinks as boundaries come and go and the panel's height follows it — and when nothing is constrained it says so rather than going blank.

  **The whole day as a heat grid.** The boundary constraints panel gains a third view beside *today* and *now*: every constraint group × every half-hour, coloured by forecast flow against its limit, with this half-hour outlined. It answers “how dynamic is this” at a glance, and today's answer was mostly “it isn't” — four groups sat over their limit all 48 half-hours (SSEN_S 3.5×, SSE_SP2 3.0×, SCOTEX 2.5×, SSHARN3 1.8×) while FLOWSTH and SEIMPPR23 crossed in and out through the afternoon, which is the part actually worth watching. Groups that never come near a limit are dropped and counted in the key, so tonight it is seven rows and not seventeen. `wall.py` sends the matrix (whole percent, capped at 999, today only) with the day's rows it already fetches: about 880 bytes.

  **These are NESO's pre-action flows** — a row at 350 % is what would flow if NESO did nothing, not a boundary running at three and a half times its limit. The note under the grid says exactly that, because without it the picture reads as a catastrophe rather than as a normal Tuesday. (`grid_wall.html` 261124.9, `wall.py` 261124.1)

- **Column two gets its 34 pixels back.** The electricity|gas switch sat on a bar of its own above the column, which pushed every panel in it down the screen. It now rides in the heading of whichever panel is at the top — *Gas system now* or *Scotland → England transfer*, whichever the column is showing — and the panels start at the top of their space again. (`grid_wall.html` 261124.7)

- **A margin notice now explains itself.** “NESO margin notice · 1,150 MW short” sat above a plot whose lowest point was 14.5 GW, and nothing on the page reconciled the two. The notice itself carries the reconciliation and we were throwing it away: **the shortfall, the contingency requirement, and — the one that matters — how much generation is excluded from the margin because of system constraints.** This morning's notice: 1,150 MW short, 1,000 MW contingency requirement, **5,100 MW excluded by constraints**. All three now appear in a block under the plot, with two sentences saying what the shortfall is measured against.

  **Both figures are NESO's and both are right.** The plotted margin is NESO's derated margin — all available capacity, derated for the chance of each unit being unavailable, less forecast demand, GB as one node. It counts the 5,100 MW. NESO's operational margin does not, because that plant sits behind boundaries that are full and cannot reach the demand, and it also holds back response and reserve. So a notice can be in force while the derated margin looks healthy and loss-of-load probability stays at 0.00, which it did across the whole window.

  **On the plot**, inside the notice window, a hatched amber band hangs off the margin line — its depth is the excluded MW — with a dashed line under it for what is left, and the contingency requirement as a dotted floor. NESO publishes one figure for the whole window, so the band is that figure held flat across it, and the caption says so rather than implying a half-hourly profile. **The shortfall itself is deliberately not drawn**: it is measured against NESO's required operating margin, which is not a published series and is not what the axis shows, so putting it there would invent a comparison. It stays a figure in the block. A notice with none of those numbers in it — a capacity market notice, say — draws no block and no band. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261124.1)

  **The wall gets the same treatment, sized for it.** Its margin panel has about 20 px of spare column — measured, not guessed — so the dashboard's block will not fit. There the header line carries the two figures that resolve the contradiction (shortfall and MW constrained out; the contingency requirement would have pushed the line past the panel's width at 880 px, and it is on the plot anyway), the plot gets the same hatched band, dashed remainder and dotted contingency floor, and the full explanation is on hover. (`grid_wall.html` 261124.2)

  **And on the wall it opens out, over the page.** The notice's full explanation is behind *what this notice means* on the capacity margin panel's heading line — the panel has 2 px of slack, so a button on a row of its own would have pushed every panel below it down even while shut. It opens as a card floating over the wall at the panel's width, directly under it: nothing moves, nothing is clipped, and the wall does not scroll. It was briefly built the other way, growing the panel and pushing the panels below it down the screen, which worked but made the page scroll as well as the wall. Same text as the hover, built once, so they cannot drift apart. (`grid_wall.html` 261124.5)

- **The transfer panel follows whichever boundary is constrained.** It was pinned to the Scotland → England border, which is where the wall started — but every ETYS boundary now has the same figures behind it (`bndFlow()` for the modelled transfer, `bndState()` for the limit, the percentage and the turn-down), and the map has been showing them on hover for weeks. The panel now **follows the tightest constrained boundary** and falls back to the border when nothing is constrained. A row of buttons in its heading overrides that — **Scot → Eng** plus one per constrained boundary — and **clicking a constrained boundary on any map sets it** too, the way clicking a substation opens its panel.

  **Two things stay particular to the border, and the panel says so rather than quietly implying otherwise.** It is the only transfer NESO meters and publishes, so it is the only one whose modelled figure carries an error bar; and it is the only one with a published day-ahead forecast flow. So on the border the three KPIs are unchanged — modelled now, NESO forecast, NESO limit — while on any other boundary they are **modelled now, % of its limit, and MW being turned down behind it**, with a note that names the limit's source and states plainly that this transfer is *not* checked against a metered value because no other boundary's flow is published.

  **The long note underneath folds away.** It is collapsed by default and remembered per panel per layout. In the stacked column the panels below simply move up by what it saved (68px as it stands); the fixed-pixel layouts can't reflow that way, so the slot is resized to what it needs and the panels under it in the same column are moved with it — growing only into space the column actually has, which in the 1920 layout is about 4px, so there it just stops the note being clipped. (`grid_wall.html` 261124.1)

## 2026-11-23

- **The “at its limit” threshold now depends on whose limit it is.** The doubled, faster-flashing boundary line fired at 100 % of whatever limit we had, and those limits are not the same kind of number. On **B6 and B4** it is NESO's own day-ahead limit, and 100 % means what it says. Everywhere else it is the **ETYS 2025 seasonal capability** — a planning figure for an assumed network, which a circuit outage can move by a gigawatt — so 100 % there is as likely to be a stale limit as a stressed boundary. NESO-limit boundaries keep **100 %**; ETYS-capability ones need **115 %**.

  **The 100 % was checked against five days of our own figures rather than guessed.** `b6_log.json` holds the page's modelled B6 per half-hour; against NESO's published SCOTEX limits for the same half-hours (19–23 Nov, 214 half-hours) the ratio ran median 41 %, p90 79 %, and reached 100 % in **four half-hours — 1.9 % of the time** — peaking at 108 % in one run on the Friday evening. So 100 % is rare enough to be worth shouting about and a flat 120 % would have fired never, including on the one real excursion. The 115 % for ETYS boundaries has no such evidence behind it, and is marked in the code as the holding figure it is.

  **So the page now logs what the boundaries actually do.** Each model run already reported which boundaries it was showing as constrained; it now reports the percentage, the turn-down behind it and which limit it was measured against, and `wall.py` keeps the per-half-hour maximum per boundary in `bnd_log.json` for 60 days. In a few weeks that gives the ETYS boundaries' own distribution, and the threshold can be set from it the way the scheduled-change baseline was. Old pages posting a plain list of names still work. (`grid_wall.html` 261123.8, `wall.py` 261123.3)

- **A constrained boundary flashes, and nothing else is drawn.** Two faults with one cause: the ETYS boundaries were drawn all the time, faint, so the map carried a permanent web of dashed lines that meant nothing, and a boundary only lit up once **200 MW or more** was being turned down behind it — which hid the smaller constraints entirely. Now a boundary is drawn **only while it is constrained** (any turn-down behind it, with the modelled transfer at 90 % of its limit or more) and it **flashes**, about once every 1.4 seconds, so it catches the eye from across the room. The 200 MW figure has not been thrown away — it has become the **colour**: **amber** under it, **pink** over it, so the scale of what NESO is doing is readable at a glance rather than being a pass/fail. The tooltip says which side of the 200 MW line it is on and why that colour. Reduced motion holds them steady.

  **The layer button is gone.** “ETYS boundaries” was a layer you could switch off, which made sense when they were permanent clutter; now that a line appears only when NESO is actually constraining that boundary, it is not something to hide, and a map with the layer left off would have silently stopped reporting constraints. The key strip has one button fewer. Hovering is restricted to drawn boundaries, so an invisible line can no longer produce a tooltip over empty map. (`grid_wall.html` 261123.4)

- **The gas page's info boxes are a third shorter.** Four rows — label, figure, caption, then a separator with “yesterday” and a sparkline under it — became three: the **sparkline moved up beside the figure** and **“yesterday” moved up beside the caption**, which drops a line and the separator with it. 128px a card down to 89px, and the row of four is that much less of the page before the flow diagram. The shrinking is done in that order deliberately: on a narrow card the figure and the sparkline each give a little (container-query sizes) and the imbalance's percentage drops out below 240px, rather than the unit being clipped off the end of the number. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261123.5)

- **A pre-warning before a big scheduled change.** GB generation changes at :00 and :30, and the frequency moves with it — so a wobble on the half hour is usually the settlement boundary, not a fault. Every unit's own notification, or the level NESO has instructed, says what it will be doing at the next boundary, so the **size of the change is published half an hour ahead rather than guessed**: `wall.py` nets it across all units, keeps interconnectors separate (they mostly ramp on the hour, and mixing them in would poison the :00 picture) and names the biggest few contributors. Two minutes before a big one, a **silent** line appears in the trigger bar — *“Scheduled change · 21:30 · −1,850 MW, about 2.3 times the usual”* — so what follows is expected rather than a mystery. No tone: nothing has gone wrong.

  **“The usual” has to be earned.** Whether a change is big *for that minute of the day* cannot be answered until the figure has been logged against the frequency for weeks, so the new `/api/ramp` writes every reading into the day log beside the 15-second frequency rows, and the baseline — median and ninth decile by minute-of-hour, weekday and weekend apart, from the last 45 days, one reading per boundary (the last before it, since a notification can still change) — appears only once a bucket has enough of them. Until then the server sends no baseline and the pre-warning falls back to an absolute 1,000 MW, about the largest credible single infeed. The dashboard therefore polls once a minute whether or not the alarms are armed, since the logging is the point; the line itself only appears when they are. Two things it cannot see, and says so in the code: embedded solar, which is never notified, and a notification that is simply not followed. (`wall.py` 261123.2, server 261123.1, `grid_dashboard.html` 261123.4)

- **The alarms now say what set them off.** A tone on its own tells you something happened but not what, and the notice tier plays a tone and says nothing at all — which is the whole of what some alarms do. Every alarm that is **not already carried by a header badge** now also writes one short line into the bar of whichever page is in front: the category in its tier's colour, what happened, and how long ago, with the border flashing while it is new. It holds for **five minutes**. A louder alarm replaces it outright; an **equally loud one makes it “Multiple alarms”**, naming each; a quieter one is ignored while a louder one is up. Several infeed losses inside five minutes collapse into one line — *“3 losses in 4 min, 2,340 MW total”* — rather than overwriting each other with the latest number. Power cuts, gas, floods and rain are left out: each already has its own badge, and repeating them would crowd the line that exists for the ones nothing else shows. It appears in **all four windows** — dashboard and its sub-pages, the wall, the power-cuts page and the forecast view — shared through `localStorage` and the storage event, the path the light/dark theme already uses. **Clicking it opens the page the alarm came from** if that is not what you are already looking at, and clears it everywhere. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261123.3, `grid_wall.html` 261123.1, `powercuts_page.html` 261123.1, `forecast_view.html` 261123.1)

- **The spoken situation summary was describing rain that wasn't a threat.** It said “rain nearby: 2 light” from a count of every wet gauge in the set, at any range — so rain 13 km away and going nowhere was announced in the same words as rain at the end of the road, with no distance, no direction and no sense of whether it was coming. The summary now **leads with the threat**: rain only when the probe says it is at your location or approaching (“Moderate rain here now”, “Rain approaching from the south west, in about 20 minutes”), then the rivers above their normal level with how far the nearest is, then “No rain heading your way” when nothing is coming. Static rain is no longer described at all — it is not a threat, and that was the complaint. Three more faults went with it: the old phrasing was not a sentence (“rain nearby: 2 light” is a bare count with no noun, and the bands came out in gauge-distance order rather than heaviest first); spoken **distances ignored the mph / km-h switch**, so a page set to kilometres still heard the probe's “about 15 miles to the south-west”; and the summary timer was started and stopped by the **critical** repeat, so it only ever spoke while a crit latch was live — it now belongs to being armed, and says nothing when there is nothing to report. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261123.2)

## 2026-11-22

- **The wall's gas panels are back in column two, with a switch.** Stacking that column into an accordion broke them: `gasSidesOn()` worked out where the gas column should go by measuring the panels' own left/top/width/height, and a stacked panel no longer carries any — the box does — so the measurement found nothing and the electricity panels simply stayed where the gas ones should have been. It takes the stacked column's own box now. Selecting **Gas sources** gives back the full gas accordion — system, alarm levels, how demand is being met, balance trend, UK and Europe storage, LNG, gas for power — exactly as before. On top of that, column two now has its own **electricity | gas** switch, so the gas column can be kept up against the network map, or the electricity panels kept while reading the gas flow diagram. Changing the regional view sets it again (gas with Gas sources, electricity otherwise), so the automatic behaviour is what a view change gives back; the switch is an override until then, and it is not remembered across a reload. (`grid_wall.html` 261122.14)

- **A collapsed wall panel reports its headline item.** The same idea as the gas column's headings, applied to the boundary constraints column: **Boundary constraints** says *2 groups over the limit · peak SCOTEX 20:00, 113%* in amber, or *nothing over its limit · tightest SCOTEX 96%* in green, or *no rows for 19:00* when the feed is missing; **Movers** gives the biggest change of the last 15 minutes with its direction; **Regional net position** the biggest exporting region and how much it is sending out; **Outages** the total off and the largest single report. The line follows the data rather than only the clicks, so a panel shut an hour ago is not still showing an hour-old headline. (`grid_wall.html` 261122.12)

- **The boundary constraints panel says what the rest of the day looks like, and the column it sits in collapses.** Four changes that belong together.

  **The day, not just this half-hour.** `_constraints()` has always fetched the whole day — `WHERE Date_Time >= todayT00:00:00` — and `wall.py` then sent the wall three half-hours of it. The rest is now summarised: the day's **tightest** half-hour, and for each group the **window** it is forecast to spend over its limit, drawn on a midnight-to-midnight bar with *now* marked and a half-hour count. A **today | now** switch appears on the panel only when something is over a limit today, since otherwise there is nothing to switch to; it opens on **today**. No extra fetch, no extra API call.

  **A blank panel now says which kind of blank.** NESO publishes a row for every constraint group each half-hour whether or not anything is near its limit, so “no rows” never meant a quiet system — it meant the data had not arrived, and the panel said neither. It now shows which day the held rows cover, the last fetch and whether it worked, the last good one when it did not, and the previous fetch's windows **dimmed** so the rest of today is still readable through a feed outage. When rows are in and nothing is over a limit, a green line says so and gives the day's tightest point, so quiet and broken no longer look alike.

  **The column is an accordion.** Five panels now — Scotland → England transfer, Boundary constraints, Movers, Regional net position, Outages — each its own height, collapsing to a heading bar, with the column scrolling when the stack is taller than the screen, as the gas column does. **Scotland → England transfer** stays open at the top. The panels keep their own elements and renderers; they simply leave the absolute layout for a scrolling box, so nothing a panel does has to know about the accordion. On the 5120 × 1440 layout only — the other layouts' columns are untouched.

  **Outages and Regional net position are two panels.** They shared one panel behind a selector, with a ⤒ button that spread the region view over the two panels above it by hiding them. In an accordion that has nothing left to do, so it is gone, and the two views are separate panels. The region **transfer diagram**, which only ever appeared in that expanded mode, would have gone with it — it is now drawn in the Regional net position panel itself, with a height of its own since an accordion panel has no spare height to lend it. The layouts that still show only one of the two (dual screen 2, dual HD screen 2) keep the selector. (`wall.py` 261122.1, `grid_wall.html` 261122.9)

- **The wall's Europe gas storage panel is now the real plot.** It had been drawing its own thin summary — one line for this year, one grey line for the five-year average, four country bars — while the gas page has the proper article: the twelve months against **each** of the previous five years, the five-year band behind them, the EU's 90% target window, the days of storage at average use, the net flow, and the **Trend | By country** switch with the per-country forecasts. The wall now shows **that card**, embedded from the dashboard through a new `#embed=eustore` view rather than copied: one implementation, and a change to the plot shows up in both places at once. The copy-it alternative would have been 400 lines of drawing code duplicated, which is exactly what the alarm panel's two implementations have cost us twice this week. On the wall the card drops the UK card above it, its own title (the panel supplies one), the ⚙ GIE key button and the sources footnote — clutter at wall distance — but keeps the key chip, and the card's own message, which carries a key button of its own, is untouched, so a missing or rejected key can still be fixed from there. The frame reports its height to the panel, so switching to **By country** resizes the panel instead of cropping it, and the panel stays exactly as tall as what it holds. Collapsed, the heading keeps its one-line *EU 69.4% full · below the 5-year range* summary and the frame is not loaded at all. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261122.7, `grid_wall.html` 261122.8)

- **The gas panels keep to the left column, and stay there.** While Gas sources spans two panels, the gas panels took **both** side columns when it found them — the system, alarm and met-by panels on the left, storage, Europe, LNG and gas for power on the right — which put gas where the wall's **electricity** readouts live: interconnectors, power cuts, substation detail. Worse, whether it did that varied between a fresh page load and re-selecting the view, so the wall rearranged itself with nothing having changed, and the panels appeared to go missing until you looked across. All eight now go in **one column, on the left**, which is the side the wide view grows into; the right column keeps its electricity panels. The right is used only in a layout with no left column at all. (`grid_wall.html` 261122.7)

- **Gas panels are as tall as what they hold, and no taller.** Sharing the column's height between the open panels made a dog's breakfast of it: **Gas system now** was stretched with a third of it blank because it was told to grow into spare height, while **How gas demand is being met now** and **UK gas storage** were cut off mid-row because they were told to fit a share. Each panel now takes its **own natural height** — open one and it expands downwards to exactly what it is showing, with nothing cropped inside it — and the **column** scrolls once the stack is taller than the screen. This reverses the 160px floor and the inner scrollbars of 261122.4: the content decides the height, and only the column scrolls. One thing does need a height of its own, since a plot has no natural one: the chart boxes are fixed at 180px, where before they took whatever the panel had spare. (`grid_wall.html` 261122.6)

- **A collapsed gas panel still reports.** Shutting a panel used to hide what it knew, which made closing one a small act of blindness — and the headings were abbreviated (“Alarm levels”) so the column did not even say what was in there. Each collapsed heading now carries its **full title** and one line of headline figure on the right: **Gas alarm levels** says *no alarms* in green, or the count at the worst level and what it is — *3 warnings · supply margin, closing linepack…* — in that level's colour; **How gas demand is being met now** shows the split bar itself, since its headline is a shape rather than a number; **UK gas storage** gives the fill and the days of gas, **Europe gas storage** the EU fill and whether it is below the five-year range, **LNG** the send-out, **Gas for power** the gas generation now. **Balance trend** deliberately shows nothing: it is a 48-hour plot and does not reduce to a figure. Two details of the alarm line are worth stating: the count is of the measures **at the worst level**, not of everything above normal, so three warnings and two notices reads “3 warnings” rather than overstating it as five; and an official Gas Deficit Warning or Balancing Notification is **always named** even when a derived measure outranks it, because it is the one item on that panel that is not our own inference. (`grid_wall.html` 261122.5)

- **Both gas accordions open as many sections as you want.** Opening one panel closed another, which is the fault the chip selectors had and not an improvement on them: comparing storage against LNG, or terminals against imports, meant flipping between the two and holding the first in your head. **In the gas column**, any number of panels can now be open at once; they share the height down to a floor of 160px each, and past that the column itself scrolls, so opening a fourth doesn't squeeze the other three into illegibility. An open panel's **own title bar** closes it again — it carries the same chevron, turned — so nothing is now reachable only by closing something else, and closing them all is allowed, since **Gas system now** stays at the top whatever else is showing (it grows into spare height but never shrinks below a readable 150px). **In “How gas demand is being met now”**, each group heading toggles its own group independently and the list scrolls when the open groups don't fit; with all of them closed the split bar at the head of the panel still says what the shape of supply is. Both remember what was open — the column per layout and per side, the groups by label — and both distinguish *nothing remembered* from *deliberately all closed*. (`grid_wall.html` 261122.4)

- **The wall's gas column is one accordion, and the alarm rows fit on one line again.** The column had grown two selectors — chips to choose which panels were shown on each side — and the alarm badges had spilled onto two lines apiece to carry their new ladders. Both are gone. **Gas system now** stays fixed at the top; every other panel is a heading bar, and clicking one opens it into the height that is left, scrolling inside itself if its content is taller. One is open at a time, remembered per layout and per side, so the column no longer has to ration space between panels that are each only occasionally wanted. The **alarm rows** return to a single line: label, headroom to the next level, a short 64px position bar, value — with the threshold list moved to the row's tooltip, since on the wall it was the one part nobody reads from across the room. The two “met by” panels are **merged**: the split bar that showed the shape of supply now heads the table of entry points instead of sitting in a panel of its own, which is the pairing it was always read as. (`grid_wall.html` 261122.3)

- **The wall's “How gas demand is being met now” opens one group at a time.** The table listed every group and all twenty-odd entry points at once, so comparing two groups meant scrolling past the rest. The group rows are now buttons — opening one closes the others, with a chevron that turns and the open group's row at full strength. Exactly one is open: clicking the open one does nothing, since a panel of bare headings is less use than any group. The choice is remembered between visits by the group's **label** rather than its position, so it survives a group appearing or going when an interconnector turns round; with nothing remembered the biggest group opens. (`grid_wall.html` 261122.2)

- **Each gas alarm badge now carries its own ladder, and the calibration caveat has an end.** The strip's detail lived only in a hover tooltip, which is no use on the wall, where it is read from across the room and nothing can be hovered. Every badge gains a **position bar** — a tick at each threshold and a fill showing where the reading sits — and a line beneath giving the thresholds and the distance to the next one: *300 · 350 · 400 mcm · 66 mcm below notice*. The bar always reads **fuller is worse**: on a downward ladder, where a bigger number is the safe end (closing linepack against the forecast minimum, days of storage cover), the scale is flipped, so the strip does not have half its bars filling towards danger and half away from it. The official-notices badge has no ladder, so it gets the line without a bar. The **“first-pass levels, calibrating”** caveat becomes **“calibrating against winter 2026/27”**, driven by one constant, `GAS_LEVELS_CALIBRATING`, which also governs the wording in the alarm settings. The old phrasing gave no clue when it should come off, which is how it survived thirteen months; naming the winter states what has to happen first. The wall's own **Gas alarm levels** panel is a separate implementation in `grid_wall.html` and has had the same treatment, so the two now agree; its calibration switch is a second pair of constants, to be flipped alongside the dashboard's. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261122.6, `grid_wall.html` 261122.1)

- **A flood warning on a river that is already in spate goes red and to the top.** On the Rivers & floods page, a warning or alert whose flood area is local *and* whose own river is above its typical high now takes a red edge bracket whatever its severity, a red tint, a **“N% of normal”** tag, and first place in the list — ahead of the other local ones, which keep the blue edge. “Over 100%” is the page's existing `riverPct` measure: the level against that station's own typical range, where 100% is the typical **high**, which is the only “normal” the Environment Agency publishes per station. A flood area and a gauging station share no identifier, so the two are matched by river name — both sides stripped of “river” and punctuation, with a substring match needing four characters so that “Exe” cannot catch “Exeter” — and where several stations sit on the same river, the highest is the one quoted. A local item's visible edge is an inset shadow rather than the border, so that goes red too or the blue would cover it. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261122.4)

- **Capacity gauges on the export routes, and on the two interconnectors when they import.** National Gas publishes no export capacity at all — Moffat's `Available Capacity` object has produced nothing in 60 days, the Exit Capacity availability, constraint and daily-price subcategories are empty, and what is left there is CMP auction clearing and oversubscription data, which is commercial rather than physical. The operators publish it instead. **BBL 22 mcm/d** and **IUK 61 mcm/d** come from National Gas's own Gas Winter Outlook 2026; both are bidirectional with no separate export figure, so the same number now serves the supply side when they are importing. **Moffat is 39.5 mcm/d**, from Gas Networks Ireland's Winter Outlook 2025/26: GNI state 39 mscm/d at their 40 MJ/scm, which is 433.4 GWh/d, and on the ~39.5 MJ/m³ basis National Gas meters in that is 39.5 mcm/d. That conversion settles something: this install's log peaked at **39.6**, which read as a breach of "39" and is in fact the pipe running at its limit. GNI raised the figure from 35 before this winter and say a further revision is being finalised, so the tooltip names the source and flags it as static and under review. An idle route's pie is dimmed rather than drawn as a full red circle, which reads as a warning instead of "nothing flowing". For reference, found while checking: Moffat serves the whole island, and Northern Ireland's own entry from it at Twynholm is capped at 88.35 GWh/d — about 8 mcm/d — against an NI severe-winter peak of 118 GWh/d, so NI cannot be fed from Moffat alone on a cold day. (`grid_server.py` 261122.2, `grid_dashboard.html` 261122.3)

- **Pies on the storage sites and on domestic demand; the demand caption corrected.** Prompted by cross-checking another AI's account of a "1-in-20 cold day" against National Gas's Gas Winter Outlook 2026 and the live feed. **Each withdrawing storage site** now carries its own pie at the head of its status line in the storage column: what it is sending out now against the maximum deliverability published for it today, the same rate-against-rate measure as the LNG pies, dropped for a site that is idle or declares no deliverability. It leads the line rather than trailing it, because once a site is withdrawing the line is long enough that a trailing mark ran into the fill percentage at the column's edge. **Domestic offtake** gains a pie on the LDZ row: National Gas's forecast of today's whole-day LDZ demand, the thirteen Local Distribution Zone forecasts summed (183.2 mcm today, tracking the published LDZ actual to within 3%), against the **highest LDZ day in the published record** — 275.9 mcm on 5 January 2026, the same day GB demand as a whole peaked at 407.5. Both are gas-day totals, so the pie measures the same thing the header's gauge does and not the live rate printed beside it; the record is re-read once a day, so a new one raises the bar by itself. Andy chose the observed record over the Winter Outlook's 1-in-20 NDM peak of 315 mcm/d, which covers a different set of customers. **The header caption changes** from "a 1-in-20 cold day" to "severe-winter demand": `Demand - Cold` is what demand would be on *this date* in a 1-in-20 cold winter, not the 1-in-20 **peak day** of 493 mcm/d in the Winter Outlook, which is the coldest day of such a winter. The two are far apart — the daily figure peaked at 396.6 last winter while demand actually reached 407.5 on 5 January — and the old wording invited the stronger reading. (`grid_server.py` 261122.1, `grid_dashboard.html` 261122.2)

- **The second scrollbar, on the dashboard's own sub-pages.** The wall's embeds were cured on 19 Nov; the dashboard itself still had it. Gas, Rivers & floods, My home and its colourgrammes, the network map and the generation and frequency histories are each a `position:fixed` overlay that scrolls inside itself, but the dashboard underneath keeps its full height, so the window kept a scrollbar of its own. It read as broken rather than untidy: dragging that bar scrolled the dashboard hidden behind the overlay, so nothing appeared to move, while the wheel over the page scrolled the overlay as expected. The document is now locked (`html.ovl-lock`) while any overlay is open. One `MutationObserver` per `*-overlay` element watches for the `open` class, so all eleven are covered and a new one is picked up without touching the code; the scroll position is taken before the lock and restored after, since the browser clamps it to the locked height. Checked on gas, floods, network, colourgrammes and the generation history: the document locks and unlocks, the overlay's own scroller still works, and the dashboard is back where it was on close. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261122.1)

## 2026-11-21

- **The storage forecast stops claiming more than it knows.** The central line is the median of six replayed winters: worth having for a few weeks, a guess at five months. It is now **drawn only to +3 months** and past that only the **band** carries on, which is the honest statement of what is actually known; the minimum marker and the level-crossing dots stop with it, since they sit on the line. The dated **“empty”** and **“under 5%”** flags are a stronger claim again, so they are only shown within **+2 months**, matching the horizon `gas_storage._fc_alerts` already uses for the warning levels — Austria’s “empty 20 Mar”, 120 days out with a band of 0–40%, is gone. The **forecast minimum** column is left blank when the minimum falls beyond the line’s horizon rather than quoting a figure the chart refuses to draw, and its heading follows the season: *forecast minimum* through the drawdown, *forecast maximum* in spring once most countries are filling. The central figure is also **floored at 0%** — the analogues can imply more withdrawal than there is gas, which printed as France −1.3% and Slovakia −0.9%; a store cannot go below empty, and that it runs out is already said by the band and the empty flag. All of it is in the page, so the server payload and the daily report are unchanged. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261121.3)

- **The supply gauges checked against the same question — and left alone.** The demand gauge's fault was dividing a flow rate by a day's volume, so the supply pies were checked the same way against this install's log for 11–21 Nov. **LNG and Langeled need no change**: their flow is near-constant, with the day's peak within about 3% of its average (Langeled 75.8 peak against a 73.6 average on 16 Nov), so the spot rate and the day average are effectively the same number and the gauge is honest as it stands. **Storage is different but still right**: it is a swing resource that pushes hard for a few hours and then sits idle, so peak-to-average ratios run from 2× to 7× (Aldbrough averaged 3.0 against a 20.6 peak on 12 Nov), and “how hard is storage pushing right now” is a live question that wants the live rate. What was wrong was the explanation, not the arithmetic: the tooltip claimed a store's declared deliverability falls as it empties, and it does not — Grain published 73.3 every single day of the window and Stublach stepped between 25.5 and 16.6 in a way that looks like plant availability rather than stock. The figure is a maximum for the whole gas day, which a site can surge past for a few hours: Hornsea peaked at 132% of its declared figure on 20 Nov and Stublach at 117% on 13 and 19 Nov, while both sat near 65% across the day. Tooltip, README and the code comments now say that. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261121.2, `grid_server.py` 261121.2)

- **The demand gauge was comparing a flow rate with a day's volume.** It divided the live instantaneous demand by National Gas's 1-in-20 cold-day figure, and those are not the same kind of quantity: the instantaneous total is a flow *rate* that happens to be quoted in mcm/d — "if this held for 24 hours" — while the cold-day figure is mscm for the whole gas day, like `Demand Actual, NTS` and `Demand Forecast, NTS`. Integrating the instantaneous total across each complete gas day in this install's own `gas_flows-2026-11.jsonl` matches the published daily actual to within **0.7%** over ten days, so the quantity is right once averaged — but a single sample is not. Demand swings 27–63% of its own daily mean through a day, so on 20 Nov, a day that finished at **84%** of the cold figure, the gauge would have read anywhere from **67% to 99%** depending on the hour; at 17:30 on 21 Nov the live rate was 297.8 against a day forecast of 249.4, which would have shown a near-full red pie on an ordinary Saturday. The pie now divides **National Gas's hourly-revised forecast of today's total** (`PUBOB28`, within 1–2 mcm of the eventual actual; `PUBOB623` as a fallback) by the cold-day total, both gas-day volumes on the same basis, and the caption reads **"today N% of a 1-in-20 cold day"** so it is not mistaken for the live figure beside it. All four references must carry the same gas day or the gauge is withheld. (`grid_server.py` 261121.1, `grid_dashboard.html` 261121.1)

## 2026-11-20

- **The mph / km-h switch now governs distances too.** With **mph** chosen, every distance on the Forecast view still read in kilometres — the range rings, the heads-up marker beyond the plot, each tracked cell's range, *nearest wet*, *misses by*, the hover cards and the one spoken line that carries a distance. The switch only ever converted speeds, which made it look broken rather than incomplete. It is now one switch over one system of units: **mph gives miles, km/h gives kilometres**, everywhere on the page and in what is spoken. The rings become **3 / 12 / 25 miles** (Andy's choice, 20 Nov) — the nearest round values to today's 5 / 20 / 40 km, with the 25 mile ring clamped to the rim it already coincides with. Speech follows the pattern the speeds already used: `rain_probe.py` writes the `wide_watch` distance in miles, rounded to the nearest 5 as it was to the nearest 5 km, and the page rewrites it to kilometres on the way to the speech engine when km/h is selected, so the quoted line always matches what will be said. The server's own figures stay in kilometres throughout; only what is printed changes. (`forecast_view.html` 261120.1, `rain_probe.py` 261120.1)

- **The demand total is gauged against a 1-in-20 cold day.** The demand side cannot have gauges row by row and the reason is not a missing feed: LDZ offtake, power stations and industrial demand are national *categories* rather than metered points, so there is nothing to divide by; no injection capacity is published for storage; Moffat's "Available Capacity" returns nothing over 33 days, like every other Available Capacity object; and the Exit Capacity publications that do carry figures are CMP auction clearing prices and oversubscription volumes — commercial, not physical. What National Gas does publish daily, for today's own date, is what demand **would** be on a 1-in-20 cold day, which is a real denominator for the total: a pie beside the **DEMAND & EXPORTS** figure with "88% of a 1-in-20 cold day" under it, so the headroom before a peak day is readable at a glance. Hovering gives the cold, seasonal-normal and warm figures together (today: 323.3 / 254.1 / 193.2 mcm/d). The *including* interconnector-and-storage variants are used, matching what the diagram's total is. Past 100% the pie fills and the rim and caption turn amber. (`grid_server.py` 261120.3, `grid_dashboard.html` 261120.3)

- **Capacity gauges on the gas flow diagram.** Each supply source on the left of the flow diagram now carries a small two-colour pie in a column of its own, left of the flow figure: green for what is flowing, red for the capacity left unused. The hard part was finding a denominator that is not a lie. National Gas's "Available Capacity" publications — all 25 of them — return nothing at all over a 33-day window; its "Entry Capacity Baseline" publishes daily for seven ASEPs but is *registered commercial* capacity rather than a physical limit, and it is per-ASEP while the diagram draws the sub-terminals separately (St Fergus would read ~55%, Bacton ~28%), so it is not used; and Easington, the ASEP Langeled lands at, publishes no baseline at all. Two figures survive, and only these get a gauge: **daily deliverability** per LNG terminal and storage site (the publications `gas_probe.py` already fetches for Expected Available Supply — live, physical, 1:1 with the diagram's nodes), and **Langeled at a fixed 80 mcm/d**, from the pipeline's own project paper (Hillenbrand, Kalwa, Schröder & Kvaale, *Experiences with an offshore pipeline project for the North Sea (Langeled)*, TMS 2007, p. 387). That paper gives 70 MSCM/d for Langeled South and 80 for Langeled North; 70 is already exceeded in this install's own flow log (75.8 mcm/d), because Langeled South also takes injections at Sleipner, so 80 is the only one of the two that holds. Everything else shows a faint dash, which says *looked, nothing published* rather than looking unfinished. Grain's two berths share one published deliverability, so a group's capacities are summed **de-duplicated by key** and Grain is not counted twice; a group whose members do not all have a capacity, and are not all idle, gets no gauge rather than a flattering one. Deliverability is a figure for the day and falls as a store empties, so a site's own flow can pass it: the wedge fills and gains an amber rim instead of wrapping, and the tooltip says so. The gauge column is folded into the label block's measured width, so the storage, LNG and terminal columns' lines still end clear of it and nothing runs under a gauge. (`grid_server.py` 261120.2, `grid_dashboard.html` 261120.2)

- **Infeed-loss alarms checked against frequency; a "False alert" flag when they don't hold up.** A drop of 800 MW or more between two 5-min FUELINST readings still raises red and the "Sudden infeed loss" card at once. It is then checked against the 15 s frequency from the previous reading −5 min to this reading +1 min, usually about 2 min after the alarm. **Confirmed** if one source makes up ≥ 60% of the drop and frequency fell (one 15 s step ≥ 0.08 Hz, or a 1-min mean to next-1-min minimum dip ≥ 0.12 Hz). Otherwise **false**, with a reason: data glitch (wind, solar or "other" fell ≥ 300 MW in the same reading, or ≥ 70% of the output was back at the next reading), schedule change (spread over several sources or interconnectors, e.g. the hourly interconnector ramps), or no frequency response. With no usable frequency within 8 min it stays unverified and nothing is shown. A false verdict drops the risk level straight away instead of waiting out the 150 s release, and adds a "False alert" notice card (tag RISK) for 15 min, so the alert history shows the withdrawal right after the alarm. Each verdict is written to the data log as a `"k":"lv"` row. Tuned on 21 Oct – 20 Nov: of 41 severe flags, 37 come out false (28 schedule, 5 glitch including 19 Nov 10:50 and 13:40, 4 no frequency response) and 4 confirmed (5 Nov 09:30 and 09:35, 11 Nov 20:30, 17 Nov 23:20). `LOSS_*` constants; `system_risk.loss_event` in the snapshot. (`grid_server.py` 261120.1)
- **Risk panel: the infeed-loss flag shows the verdict.** After the flashing alarm the small flag reads "checking frequency…" (amber), then either **FALSE ALERT** · time, MW, source and reason (blue, 15 min; the tooltip gives the largest 15 s fall) or "✓ confirmed" with how far frequency fell. A false verdict does not pip. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261120.1)

## 2026-11-19

- **Two scrollbars on a single wall panel.** The gas panel on the wall (`#embed=gas`, and the same for ea, gen, gh and octo) had one scrollbar that scrolled the panel and another, down the very right edge, that appeared to do nothing. Each of those embeds is a fixed overlay that scrolls inside itself, but the dashboard behind it is only hidden, not removed, so the document stayed as tall as the whole dashboard and scrolled too. These modes now lock the document the way `#embed=freq`, `#embed=gasflow` and `#embed=wx` always did — which is why those panels never showed it. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261119.1)

- **The approach line says why it is quiet.** In showery weather the spoken alerts were sparse and there was no way to tell a working engine from a broken one. The engine already decides, every poll, whether a threat may speak and records the gate that stopped it — that verdict now reaches the Forecast view as an **approach** row: *withheld: isolated beyond 12 km — 18 km south-west · 0.9 mm/h · isolated · misses by 8.9 km · in 15 to 24 minutes*. The threat's `along_km` and `miss_km` travel with it, so a shower dropped by the new closest-approach test is visible as such. No gate or threshold has changed. (`rain_probe.py` 261119.2, `forecast_view.html` 261119.7)

- **The radar plot's labels stand up to a bright picture.** Every label drawn on the open plot — the ring distances, cell ids and tags, the mobile trackers, *home*, the heads-up — now sits on its own dark plate, so it stays readable over heavy rain or satellite imagery instead of disappearing into it. A **cell's tag runs to two lines** when it has a source to name: the motion on the first (`→ E · 40 mph`) and where it came from on the second (`radar, unconfirmed`), which keeps a long tag narrow instead of stretching it across the plot — the id moves up to sit above both. The **eight spokes now carry a little past the rim** and are labelled **N NE E SE S SW W NW**, the stub outside the circle drawn brighter than the part that crosses the rain. A cardinal that would land on a cell's label is **muted and loses its plate** rather than printing over the reading. (`forecast_view.html` 261119.3)

- **The approach line stopped counting rain that was never coming.** At 00:08 it read *very heavy rain, 20 km to the south, in 9 min*; five minutes later, *moderate, 35 km to the south-west, in 34 min*. The second was right and the first was wrong. The engine gathered every wet cell within ±60° of the steering flow and took the **nearest** as the threat, timing it as though it were coming straight here — but a cell at the cone's edge passes 0.87 × its range to one side, so the 20 km shower to the south was always going to miss by 17 km, and the line lurched backwards when it tracked out of the cone and the real band behind it took over. Every cell is now carried along the flow: **`along`**, how far it still has to travel to its closest approach here, and **`miss_km`**, how wide that approach is. The threat is the first to **arrive**, not the nearest, and anything already past or missing by more than **12 km + 15% of its range** is not counted at all — so it no longer sets the distance, the direction, the arrival or the intensity. The allowance widens with range because a band 40 km out is sampled coarsely and is wide: a point 18° off the axis at 40 km still counts, a shower 43° off at 28 km does not. (`rain_probe.py` 261119.1)

## 2026-11-18

- **A switchable background map under the radar.** A `map:` button under the unit pill cycles **off → OSM → dark → light → toner → satellite**, remembered between visits. OSM standard needs no key and works wherever the page is opened from; the other four are Stadia (Alidade Smooth Dark and its light counterpart, Stamen Toner Lite and Alidade Satellite), which serve a page on **localhost** without a key — opened from another machine on the LAN they answer 401, and the button then reads *needs a key here* rather than failing silently. The tiles are fetched by the browser as images, so they carry a real browser's User-Agent and Referer and use its own cache: nothing is written to disk and nothing is pre-fetched, which is what OpenStreetMap's tile policy asks for. Only the tiles on screen are requested — about **20 at zoom 10** for the full-page plot, chosen to match the plot's own scale — and each is placed by its own corners in degrees converted to the plot's flat km space, so Mercator's stretch is carried rather than assumed away. Each layer has its own opacity so the rain stays the brightest thing on the plot, the map is clipped to the 40 km circle like everything else, and the provider's credit sits in the key block where it cannot be hidden. (`forecast_view.html` 261118.8)

- **Rain-rate bars go white where the echo is more likely hail.** Above **55 dBZ** — the threshold the radar plot already paints white, about 100 mm/h read as rain — an echo is better explained by hail than by rain or snow, but the bars carried on up the rain scale into magenta as though it were simply a downpour. The part of a bar above that line is now white on both rate plots (the radar view's **max rain rate in plot** and the dashboard's **Precipitation at home**), whatever the rain/snow colours below it, and the hover says **possible hail** in place of rain or snow. A hint, not a detection, exactly as the radar plot's white cells are. (`forecast_view.html` 261118.5, `grid_dashboard.html` 261118.6)

- **A heads-up from beyond the plot.** The coarse surround reaches 100 km, about 60 km further upwind than anything else the engine watches, so it can see a front that is still well outside the 40 km circle — at 50 km/h that is a little over an hour of warning on top of the plot's own. Each poll walks the surround outward along the measured motion, within 60° of upwind, and reports the nearest ring holding **150 km² of rain with more behind it**, so a stray cell can't raise it: distance, bearing, band, the closing speed and a rough arrival. The radar view marks it with an amber chevron on the rim at its bearing, gives the detail on hover and a `heads-up (100 km)` row in the situation table, and it can speak — but only when nothing nearer is being announced and nothing is falling at home, since it is the furthest-out and least certain signal there is. It is **translation only**, like everything else here: a band 90 km out can decay or veer, so it reads as a watch, the spoken time is rounded to the nearest five minutes under an hour and to half-hours beyond, and it says nothing at all past three hours or where the band is not closing. (`rain_probe.py` 261118.2, `forecast_view.html` 261118.4)

- **The radar frame no longer draws a straight edge across the circle.** The view moves the newest frame on to the current time, and the animation carries it up to an hour further, so the fine grid — 40 km half-width, exactly tangent to the 40 km circle — drifted its own edge inside the plot and ruled a line across it. Every frame now carries a **coarse surround**: a second cut of the same tiles out to **100 km at 2 km cells**, drawn underneath and clipped to what the fine grid has drifted off, so the crescent it vacates shows blockier rain instead of nothing. 100 km covers an hour's drift at 60 km/h. It costs about 10 kB a frame against the fine grid's 26 kB and **no extra tile fetches worth counting** — RainViewer's tiles here are zoom 7 at 512 px, around 200 km across, so the wider cut comes from the same handful already fetched. The surround is clipped out of the fine grid's own footprint rather than drawn under it, because the fine cells are deliberately translucent and would otherwise take colour from it. Recorded frames whose saved tiles do not reach that far go without one, as they did before. (`rain_probe.py` 261118.1, `forecast_view.html` 261118.3)

- **The gas page carries its own alert panel.** The gas-domain alerts — official National Gas notices (NG), the derived alarm ladder (GAS), stale storage figures (DATA) and **the European storage forecast (STORE)** — now appear at the top of the gas page as well as in the bar at the top of the dashboard, so a country's storage running down is in front of you while you are reading the storage tables rather than scrolled off above them. It behaves like the flood warnings on the EA page: the worst one open, the rest behind **show N more**, one body open at a time, severity down the left edge. Same list and same data as the main bar, so a dismissal there clears it here too. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261118.4)

- **“Gas margin tight” joins that panel.** The derived margin signal — demand over supply by 10% or more, so linepack is drawing down — had a banner of its own below the alerts. It is now one item in the list, a watch with a **derived signal** tag, carrying the same figures, the same largest-demand line and the same small print about it not being an official Margins Notice. It sorts in with the server's alerts worst-first, so a Gas Deficit Warning still sits above it. The banner and its styles are gone. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261118.5)

- **Speeds switch between mph and km/h.** The weather page mixed km/h on the radar's own lines, mph on the cell tracks and gauges, and m/s beside them. A **mph | km/h** pill now sits under the nowcast box in the radar's top-left corner and carries the lot: the radar's motion and 850 hPa lines, the cell labels, tooltips and tracked-cells list, the mobile trackers, the steering-wind row, and below the radar the wind gauge, its gusts and 850 hPa reading, the radar-track caption and the rain-nearby speeds. **m/s is left exactly where it was**, as the second figure on the gauges. Spoken alerts follow the switch too — the server writes them in miles per hour and they are rewritten on the way to the speech engine. The radar is an embedded page, so the setting is shared by localStorage and a message each way between the frame and the page around it; the two can't disagree. **The speed gauge keeps its face**: the log curve, the needle and the gust and steering ticks are still computed in mph, so nothing moves when the unit changes — only the numbers and the tick marks, drawn in km/h at 10, 20, 40, 60, 100 and 160 against the same scale. The power-cuts page has a switch of its own (**kt | mph | km/h**, knots by default, since METAR publishes knots and the learned curve is binned in them) above the wind-vs-cuts plot; the bins are untouched and only the labels and the live gust convert. (`forecast_view.html` 261118.1, `grid_dashboard.html` 261118.2, `powercuts_page.html` 261118.1)

- **Rain arriving soon counts down in seconds.** The nowcast headline's “· in 21 min” held whole minutes and refreshed every 15 s. Inside ten minutes it now reads **· in 4:35** and ticks every second — but only where the estimate earns that precision: a measured approach whose window is a quarter of an hour or less, or OpenWeather's minute nowcast. A wind-estimated or wide-window arrival keeps whole minutes on the old 15 s refresh. The countdown also runs to the time actually on the line (the middle of a narrow window, the start of a wide one) rather than to the window's start regardless, and “any minute” now waits for the unrounded earliest time to pass instead of the 5-minute-rounded one, which used to swallow the last two minutes of the count. The 1 s redraw runs only while such a countdown is on screen and the tab is visible. (`forecast_view.html` 261118.1)

## 2026-11-16

- **Radar animation: 5-second pause on the final frame.** The loop holds for 5 s on its last frame (the newest radar frame, or the end of the projection when there is one) before starting again; it held 1.8 s after a projection and not at all without one. `AN_END_HOLD`. (`forecast_view.html` 261116.1)
- **Rain alerts tuned from the rain recorder (30 Sep – 16 Nov).** Approach alerts checked against radar at home: 22 of 38 were followed by rain, arrival windows ran early. (1) An approach alert is withheld unless the newest radar frame (≤ 20 min old) shows rain ≥ 0.1 mm/h 5–30 km out within ±35° of the threat's bearing (`radar_sector_mm`, `APPR_GATE_RADAR_*`); no usable frame, no gate. On the logged alerts this drops 8, all false (58% → 73% right), mostly EA-gauge threats ~30 min old saying "any time now". (2) Arrival windows from the 850 hPa steering wind span 0.45–0.85× its speed (`STEER_ETA_SLOW` / `STEER_ETA_FAST`, slow edge capped at 150 min; reach still set by the fast edge against `ETA_MAX_MIN`) instead of ±20% around 1×: rain arrived at a median 0.6× (IQR 0.3–0.8×); windows holding the arrival 6 → 12 of 20. "Within the next fifteen minutes" only for a window ending by 15 min or ≤ 15 min wide. (3) Radar watch: no alert within 30 min of radar rain at home (`RW_AFTER_WET_S`, `rw_home_wet_ts`); a weakening track says "It is weakening, but may reach you …" with its time ("Most is likely to die out" was wrong 3 of 4). EA publication lag checked: median 29 min, p95 47 min — late/age limits unchanged. (`rain_probe.py` 261116.1)
- **Storage forecast: warning levels at 30%, 11% and 5%, Germany's legal minimum, and the EU refill target.** The single 5% level (watch within 93 days, alarm within 31) is replaced by three levels, each with a base tier: 30% where the withdrawal rate starts to fall as a store's pressure drops (watch), the 11% strategic reserve that ENTSOG's Winter Supply Outlook 2026/27 treats as the floor (alert), and 5%, near empty (alarm). A level is at its base tier when the central forecast goes under it within 62 days, one tier higher within 31 days or when the store is already under it (and not filling); the band alone going under within 31 days is a watch. Germany's legal 30% on 1 Feb 2027 (Bundesnetzagentur) is checked within 120 days of the date: an alert if the central line is under it, a watch if only the band is. The EU 90% target (1 Oct – 1 Dec, less a 10-point allowance) is checked against the projected peak in the next window, but counts only once the winter's low has passed; until then the tooltip shows it as an outlook. Each country's forecast now carries `alerts` (worst first) and `cross` (first day under each level); `state` is ok / watch / alert / alarm. With 14 Nov data: Germany alert (28% on 1 Feb against its 30% minimum), Slovakia watch (under 30% by 29 Dec), Belgium watch (band under 30% by 15 Dec), the rest clear. (`gas_storage.py` 261116.1)
- **Gas alert bar: storage forecast from the new levels.** "Gas storage heading low" (notice) for a watch, "Gas storage forecast to run low" (warning) for an alert or alarm, titles unchanged so the alert history still matches. The detail names up to four countries with what triggered each, e.g. "Germany 28% on 1 Feb (band 9–53%) vs 30% German legal minimum". (`grid_server.py` 261116.1)
- **Storage forecast plot and table: the new levels drawn.** Dashed lines at 30% (watch) and 11% (alert) as well as 5% (alarm), a dot and date where the central line first goes under 30% and 11%, and a diamond at a dated legal minimum (Germany 30% on 1 Feb) in its tier colour. The forecast column is coloured by the worst state (amber watch, orange alert, red alarm; a minimum under 5% beyond the warning horizon in amber), and its tooltip lists each trigger. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261116.2)
- **Storage forecast: the central line is dashed once it stops meaning much.** On each country's forecast plot the line is solid while the band is within ±15 points of it (the wider side, as the band is clipped at 0 and 100) and dashed at half strength from the first point where it is wider. At ±15 the band can no longer tell the 30% and 11% levels apart. A forecast with no band is dashed from 30 days after its start. The stretch under 5% follows the same rule, and the subtitle says where the dashes begin. With 14 Nov data the dashes begin from early December (Poland, Belgium, France) to early February (Italy). `GSV_FC_SOLID_PTS` / `GSV_FC_SOLID_D` set the two limits. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261116.1)

## 2026-11-15

- **Wall, two-panel gas view: the LNG cargo lanes are back.** Expanded on the wall, the LNG section left only 205 units above the first terminal and 60 between terminals, which was the room the first cargo strip needed. The rebuilt column (cargo lanes hanging level with the nodes, and the tanks box grown to carry send-out, the month's tank gain and the stock sparkline) needs more, so there it fell back to its text-only rows: no CARGO ARRIVALS lanes and no tank detail. The section now puts the first terminal 250 below its top (still 60 apart), enough for the lanes to hang from it. The tanks box keeps its short form there: growing it for its detail needed 400 and made the whole drawing, which the panel scales to its height, about 15% smaller (261115.1 did that; 261115.2 takes it back). (`grid_dashboard.html` 261115.1, 261115.2)
- **Radar animation: projection muted when the motion is uncertain; a button per span.** Reverses the 261115.5 fallback: when the live motion can't be determined (its frame pairs disagree, or it is unknown) the projection is left out rather than run on recent drift, and the panel says why ("no projection: motion uncertain"). The loop length is now four buttons, **2h 6h 12h 24h**, the chosen one lit, beside play and speed (on a narrow panel they take a row of their own). (`forecast_view.html` 261115.6)
- **Radar animation: the projection runs when the live motion is uncertain.** It was shown only when the live view's motion was usable, so on an evening like this one (the last frame pairs disagree) the projected frames disappeared. It now falls back to the median of the last three measured frame-pair drifts within 30 minutes, and the key says so ("recent drift; live motion uncertain"). With no measured drift (near-dry radar), or with the key set to "as measured", there is still no projection. (`forecast_view.html` 261115.5)
- **Radar animation: each step slides along the drift at the time.** The slide between two frames used the motion measured now, so the older frames moved the wrong way when the drift had changed (this evening it swung from ESE at ~50 km/h to almost still). The server now measures the drift between each pair of frames, the way the live view measures it, and sends it with the frames; a pair with too little rain or no clear match borrows the median of the pairs within two steps either side (shown with ~), and with none it cross-fades without sliding. The key shows the drift then ("drift then 48 km/h → 142°"). The projection still uses the current motion. Measuring adds about 4 s to a first 24 h load (once per pair, then kept). Needs a server restart. (`forecast_view.html` 261115.4, `rain_probe.py` 261115.3)
- **Radar animation: no pause at the current time.** The loop runs straight through the newest frame; it holds only at the end of a projection before starting again. (`forecast_view.html` 261115.3)
- **Radar animation: from the recorded frames, up to 24 hours.** The animation now takes its frames from the ones the rain recorder has already saved (`rain_rec/radar/`, every RainViewer frame's tiles since it started), so they cost nothing to fetch, and a new **2 h / 6 h / 12 h / 24 h** button (remembered) sets how far back the loop runs. RainViewer is asked only for a frame the recorder doesn't have, while RainViewer still lists it; a frame neither has is left out rather than drawn part-dry. Speeds now 1×, 2×, 4×, 8×, ½×; long loops read in hours (−5 h 20 min) with the day on the timeline. Building 24 hours the first time takes about 12 s; after that only a new frame is built. Needs a server restart. (`forecast_view.html` 261115.2, `rain_probe.py` 261115.2, `grid_server.py` 261115.2)
- **Radar view: ▶ animate radar.** A button at the top right of the radar plot (the Forecast view, the EA page's radar and the wall's) plays the last ~2 hours of RainViewer frames (13, 10 minutes apart) in place of the live picture, sliding and cross-fading between frames along the radar's measured motion so it moves smoothly rather than jumping every 10 minutes. When the motion is usable (the same rule as "moved to now"), the newest frame is then carried on for another hour, in amber with a dashed ring and marked PROJECTED: movement only, no growth or decay. Pause / play, speed (1×, 2×, 4×, ½×), ■ live to go back, and the timeline under them scrubs. The key's time line follows the animation. The gauges and tracks stay as they are now. Frames come from a new `/api/radar-frames`: each frame's grid is built once from its own tiles and kept (the first request fetches about 13-26 tiles, then 1-2 per new frame, only while someone is animating; metered as `radar_tile_anim`), on the rain probe's own lock, so it never holds up the engine. Needs a server restart. (`forecast_view.html` 261115.1, `rain_probe.py` 261115.1, `grid_server.py` 261115.1)
- **Frequency histogram: bars fade with age.** Each bar is drawn in one shade, set by the age of the newest reading in it: brighter than its band colour (mixed 45% towards white) when that reading is new, with the brightening and the opacity falling linearly to 0% at the end of the period chosen with the 1h-24h buttons. Bar heights, hover figures and the buttons are unchanged. The histogram is redrawn every 15 s, once per reading interval (it was every minute), so the fade moves in steps of 15 s / period, 0.4% at 1 h and less at longer periods, too small to see. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261115.3, 261115.5)
- **Wall, two-panel gas view: larger text in the side columns.** The St Fergus, storage and LNG columns' text is drawn 25% larger when expanded on the wall (headline figures excepted), with the columns widened from 820 to 900 to give it room (the diagram part narrows to suit, so the drawing's scale is unchanged). The storage rows' bar, % and detail move right to suit, and the cargo lanes' date axis drops clear of the lozenges. (`grid_dashboard.html` 261115.2)

## 2026-11-14

- **Storage forecast: says when it is extrapolating, instead of printing a confident number.** The projection subtracts a past winter's drawdown, measured in percentage points, from today's level. That interpolates safely while today's level sits inside the range of that country's own past starts — and runs to the floor by construction when it does not, because a drawdown measured from a much fuller store is being taken off a much emptier one. **Six of the twelve are in that position this winter**: Slovakia starts 18 points below anything in its six-year record, Belgium 17, Germany 13, Czechia 11, France 10, Spain 2 — the same story ENTSOG tells about Europe entering winter at 72% against 83%. Those rows now carry a **~** and their band is widened to span a second reading of the same winters, scaled (today's level × that winter's own proportion of its start) rather than shifted — Slovakia reads 0.0–45.5% rather than a flat 1.2%. The tooltip, the plot's sub-line and the STORE alert all say so. The central line is unchanged: across the 36 in-range country-years the shifted method still backtests better than the scaled one (median error 7.6 points against 8.5, worst 25.8 against 49.8), so this widens the stated uncertainty rather than moving the estimate. The quoted backtest accuracy now says it covers in-range countries only. (`gas_storage.py` 261114.4, `grid_dashboard.html` 261114.9, `grid_server.py` 261114.2)

- **Europe gas storage: a forward forecast per country, with a minimum date, a band and an alarm.** Each country's GIE history now goes back to **1 Oct 2020** rather than 400 days — six winters instead of one — fetched newest-first in chunks within a time budget per refresh, resuming after a stop. The forecast replays each of those winters from today's level as a day-by-day change in % full, so the real shape of a winter carries through **including its spring refill**; each analogue's withdrawals (not its injections) are scaled by how this season's drawdown so far compares with the same stretch of that year. The median is the central line and the spread is the band, padded by 10 × √(days/130). **The by-country table gains a `forecast minimum` column** (date and %, with the bars narrowed to fit), and **a country name opens its forecast plot** below the table with the uncertainty band, the 5% line and the minimum marked. The bottom of a winter is flat for weeks, so the date shown is the **earliest** day within a point of the minimum, with the whole window, the band and the analogue years on hover. When a store is filling there is no minimum ahead, so the **peak of the refill** is shown instead. A new **STORE** alert warns when a country's projection goes under 5% within three months and raises it to a warning within a month; it dismisses like any other. Backtested on 36 country-years of forecasts made on 12 November: median error **10 days** on the date and **9.4 points** on the level, with the band covering what happened **89–100%** of the time at every horizon from a week to five months. (`gas_storage.py` 261114.3, `grid_dashboard.html` 261114.8, `grid_server.py` 261114.1)

## 2026-11-12

- **Gas page: the LNG tanks box carries the month as well as the moment.** With the cargo lanes sitting level with the terminals there was a gap under the tanks box, so the box grows into it with three more readings, all from figures already fetched: **send-out now** across the three terminals with its share of UK supply; **into the tanks**, the window's total tank gain against the 31 days before it (7.76 TWh against 4.38 as this went in — LNG arrivals nearly doubled month on month); and **tank stock** as a sparkline over the same 31 gas days as the lanes, with its range and net change.
